<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Government_of_Australia" title="Government of Australia"><img alt="Government of Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/40px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="31" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/60px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/80px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="397" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Malcolm_Turnbull_sidebar" title="Template:Malcolm Turnbull sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Malcolm_Turnbull_sidebar" title="Template talk:Malcolm Turnbull sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Malcolm_Turnbull_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Malcolm Bligh Turnbull</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">AC</a></span> (born 24 October 1954) is an Australian former politician and businessman who served as the 29th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a> from 2015 to 2018. He held office as leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>.
</p><p>Turnbull graduated from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a> as a Bachelor of Arts and a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws" title="Bachelor of Laws">Bachelor of Laws</a>, before attending <a href="/wiki/Brasenose_College,_Oxford" title="Brasenose College, Oxford">Brasenose College, Oxford</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship" title="Rhodes Scholarship">Rhodes Scholar</a>, earning a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Civil_Law" title="Bachelor of Civil Law">Bachelor of Civil Law</a> degree. For more than two decades, he worked as a journalist, lawyer, <a href="/wiki/Merchant_bank" title="Merchant bank">merchant banker</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Venture_capital" title="Venture capital">venture capitalist</a>. He was Chair of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Republic_Movement" title="Australian Republic Movement">Australian Republican Movement</a> from 1993 to 2000, and was one of the leaders of the unsuccessful "Yes" campaign in the <a href="/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum" title="1999 Australian republic referendum">1999 republic referendum</a>. He was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_parliament" title="Member of parliament">member of parliament</a> (MP) for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wentworth" title="Division of Wentworth">division of Wentworth</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2004_Australian_federal_election" title="2004 Australian federal election">2004 election</a>, and was <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_the_Environment_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of the Environment (Australia)">Minister for the Environment and Water</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a> from January 2007 until December 2007.
</p><p>After coming second in the <a href="/wiki/2007_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="2007 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">2007 leadership election</a>, Turnbull won the leadership of the Liberal Party in a <a href="/wiki/2008_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="2008 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">leadership spill</a> the following year and became <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a>. However, his support of the <a href="/wiki/Carbon_Pollution_Reduction_Scheme" title="Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme">Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme</a> proposed by the <a href="/wiki/Rudd_government_(2007%E2%80%932010)" title="Rudd government (2007–2010)">Rudd government</a> in December 2009 led in turn to a <a href="/wiki/2009_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="2009 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">leadership challenge</a> by <a href="/wiki/Tony_Abbott" title="Tony Abbott">Tony Abbott</a>, who defeated Turnbull by a single vote. Though initially planning to leave politics after this, Turnbull chose to remain and was later appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Communications_(Australia)" title="Minister for Communications (Australia)">Minister for Communications</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government" title="Abbott government">Abbott government</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-National Coalition's</a> victory at the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 election</a>.
</p><p>Two years later, citing consistently poor opinion polling, Turnbull resigned from the Cabinet on 14 September 2015 and <a href="/wiki/September_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="September 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">challenged</a> Abbott, successfully reclaiming the leadership of the Liberal Party by ten votes. He was sworn in as prime minister the following day. The <a href="/wiki/Turnbull_government" title="Turnbull government">Turnbull government</a> initiated the National Innovation and Science Agenda as its key economic priority, working to promote <a href="/wiki/STEM" class="mw-redirect" title="STEM">STEM</a> education, increase <a href="/wiki/Venture_capital" title="Venture capital">venture capital</a> funding for new <a href="/wiki/Start-ups" class="mw-redirect" title="Start-ups">start-ups</a>, and launch an "ideas boom".<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull also pursued "city deals" with local and state governments to improve planning outcomes and encourage investment in major infrastructure projects such as the <a href="/wiki/Western_Sydney_Airport" title="Western Sydney Airport">Western Sydney Airport</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In 2016, Turnbull led the Coalition to a narrow victory in a <a href="/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_election" title="2016 Australian federal election">double dissolution election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-lead2_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lead2-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In his second term, Turnbull initiated and campaigned for the "Yes" side in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Marriage_Law_Postal_Survey" title="Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey">same-sex marriage plebiscite</a>, which was ultimately successful. Turnbull also announced <a href="/wiki/Snowy_Hydro_2.0" class="mw-redirect" title="Snowy Hydro 2.0">Snowy Hydro 2.0</a>, a major expansion of the <a href="/wiki/Snowy_Mountains_Scheme" title="Snowy Mountains Scheme">Snowy Mountains Scheme</a> as a key component in enabling the transition to <a href="/wiki/Renewable_energy" title="Renewable energy">renewable energy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In late 2017, the government experienced a <a href="/wiki/2017-18_Australian_parliamentary_eligibility_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="2017-18 Australian parliamentary eligibility crisis">parliamentary eligibility crisis</a> that saw fifteen parliamentarians forced out of Parliament due to concerns about <a href="/wiki/Dual_citizenship" class="mw-redirect" title="Dual citizenship">dual citizenship</a>. 
</p><p>To address climate change and reform energy policy, in August 2018 Turnbull proposed the <a href="/wiki/National_Energy_Guarantee" title="National Energy Guarantee">National Energy Guarantee</a>. Although initially agreed to by the Cabinet, the policy was ultimately rejected by the party room. This, combined with poor <a href="/wiki/Opinion_polls" class="mw-redirect" title="Opinion polls">opinion polling</a>, led to <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> <a href="/wiki/2018_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spills" title="2018 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills">challenging Turnbull</a> for the Liberal leadership. Although Turnbull defeated Dutton in the party room, a majority of MPs demanded a second spill, which Turnbull did not contest. On 24 August 2018, <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison" title="Scott Morrison">Scott Morrison</a> defeated Dutton and <a href="/wiki/Julie_Bishop" title="Julie Bishop">Julie Bishop</a> in the contest, and replaced Turnbull as prime minister. Turnbull resigned from Parliament, triggering a <a href="/wiki/2018_Wentworth_by-election" title="2018 Wentworth by-election">by-election in his former seat of Wentworth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal Party lost the by-election to independent candidate <a href="/wiki/Kerryn_Phelps" title="Kerryn Phelps">Kerryn Phelps</a>, which resulted in the Coalition losing its absolute majority in the House of Representatives.
</p><p>Since retiring from politics, Turnbull has become an advisor to Kasada, an Australian <a href="/wiki/Cybersecurity" class="mw-redirect" title="Cybersecurity">cybersecurity</a> start-up.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He has been critical of the direction of the Liberal Party, and has joined with fellow former prime minister <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> in criticising the dominance of <a href="/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch" title="Rupert Murdoch">Rupert Murdoch</a>'s <a href="/wiki/News_Corp_Australia" title="News Corp Australia">News Corp</a> in Australian political debate.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<p>Malcolm Bligh Turnbull was born in Sydney, on 24 October 1954, the only child of Bruce Bligh Turnbull and <a href="/wiki/Coral_Lansbury" title="Coral Lansbury">Coral Magnolia Lansbury</a>. His father was a hotel broker, while his mother was a radio actor, writer, and academic, and a second cousin of the British film and television actress <a href="/wiki/Angela_Lansbury" title="Angela Lansbury">Angela Lansbury</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-New_York_Times_obituary_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-New_York_Times_obituary-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His maternal grandmother, May Lansbury (<i>née</i> Morle), was born in England, while his other grandparents were Australian-born.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He is also of <a href="/wiki/Scottish_people" title="Scottish people">Scottish</a> descent; his great-great-great-grandfather John Turnbull (1751–1834) arrived on the <i><a href="/wiki/Coromandel_(1793_ship)" title="Coromandel (1793 ship)">Coromandel</a></i> in 1802 in <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a> and became a tailor. In an interview in 2015, Turnbull said that his <a href="/wiki/Middle_name" title="Middle name">middle name</a> "Bligh" has been a family tradition for generations, originally given in honour of Governor <a href="/wiki/William_Bligh" title="William Bligh">William Bligh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull's parents married in December 1955, fourteen months after his birth.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The Turnbulls lived in a two-bedroom flat in <a href="/wiki/Vaucluse,_New_South_Wales" title="Vaucluse, New South Wales">Vaucluse</a>, where Turnbull would attend the <a href="/wiki/Vaucluse_Public_School" title="Vaucluse Public School">nearby public school</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Bruce and Coral's marriage was an unhappy one, and Turnbull wrote that the couple rarely slept or spent time together, only staying married due to himself.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Coral frequently belittled her husband about his lack of education and that the fortunes of the family depended on her.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Coral started spending increasing amounts of time with John Salmon, a professor of history at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales" title="University of New South Wales">University of New South Wales</a>. When Turnbull was nine, Salmon received a teaching post in <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>, and Coral went with him, ending the marriage. Bruce told Turnbull that Coral was studying for another degree, which although Coral did get a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Philosophy" title="Doctor of Philosophy">PhD</a> in New Zealand, was an effort by Bruce to hide the fact that Coral had left him.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull was from then raised solely by his father.<sup id="cite_ref-ra_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ra-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-randomhouse_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-randomhouse-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull suffered from <a href="/wiki/Asthma" title="Asthma">asthma</a> as a young child.<sup id="cite_ref-TurnbullChildhoodBtR_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TurnbullChildhoodBtR-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After spending his first three years of schooling at <a href="/wiki/Vaucluse_Public_School" title="Vaucluse Public School">Vaucluse Public School</a>, He started boarding at <a href="/wiki/Sydney_Grammar_School" title="Sydney Grammar School">Sydney Grammar School</a> in <a href="/wiki/St_Ives,_New_South_Wales" title="St Ives, New South Wales">St Ives</a>. Turnbull wrote that he hated boarding school, as he was bullied due to <a href="/wiki/Bed_wetter" class="mw-redirect" title="Bed wetter">bed wetting</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Bruce, now a single father, had troubles paying school fees, causing many letters to be sent to the Turnbull residence from the school <a href="/wiki/Bursar" title="Bursar">bursar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> These financial issues forced the Turnbulls to move from Vaucluse to a small flat in <a href="/wiki/Double_Bay" class="mw-redirect" title="Double Bay">Double Bay</a>, where they lived without much furniture.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull then began to attend Grammar's high school campus on <a href="/wiki/College_Street,_Sydney" title="College Street, Sydney">College Street</a>, on a partial scholarship.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> During this time he lived at the school's former <a href="/wiki/Randwick,_New_South_Wales" title="Randwick, New South Wales">Randwick</a> boarding facilities.<sup id="cite_ref-Clune_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clune-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Q&amp;A_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Q&amp;A-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Overington_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Overington-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> During this time, Bruce remarried, and his business continued to grow. The Turnbulls moved into a large apartment in <a href="/wiki/Point_Piper" title="Point Piper">Point Piper</a>, and the pressure of the school fees was relieved.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Although a mediocre mathematician, Turnbull excelled in Greek, English, and History, and joined the debating and drama clubs, where he won the <a href="/wiki/Lawrence_Campbell_Oratory_Competition" title="Lawrence Campbell Oratory Competition">Lawrence Campbell Oratory Competition</a> and starred in numerous <a href="/wiki/William_Shakespeare" title="William Shakespeare">Shakespeare</a> plays, respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-TurnbullChildhoodBtR_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TurnbullChildhoodBtR-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Clune_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clune-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He was made senior school co-captain in 1972,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> however, contrary to certain sources, Turnbull was not the <a href="/wiki/Dux#Education" title="Dux">dux</a> of his graduating year at Sydney Grammar.<sup id="cite_ref-Overington_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Overington-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-McNicoll_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McNicoll-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In 1987, in memory of his late father, he set up the Bruce Turnbull means-tested scholarship at Sydney Grammar, offering full remission of fees to a student unable to afford them.<sup id="cite_ref-Q&amp;A_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Q&amp;A-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1973, Turnbull attended the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a>, graduating with a Bachelor of Arts in political science in 1977 and a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws" title="Bachelor of Laws">Bachelor of Laws</a> in 1978.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-smhrise_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smhrise-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull wrote for the school's newspaper, <i><a href="/wiki/Honi_Soit" title="Honi Soit">Honi Soit</a></i>, writing largely about politics.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull also visited former <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_South_Wales" title="Premier of New South Wales">Premier of New South Wales</a> <a href="/wiki/Jack_Lang_(Australian_politician)" title="Jack Lang (Australian politician)">Jack Lang</a>, discussing 1920s and 1930s state politics.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> During his studies, he was involved in student politics, serving as board director of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney_Union" title="University of Sydney Union">University of Sydney Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He also worked part-time as a political journalist for <i><a href="/wiki/Nation_Review" title="Nation Review">Nation Review</a></i>, Radio <a href="/wiki/2SM" title="2SM">2SM</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nine_Network" title="Nine Network">Channel 9</a>, covering state politics.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Around this time, Turnbull would meet businessman <a href="/wiki/Kerry_Packer" title="Kerry Packer">Kerry Packer</a>, who he would later do extensive legal work for.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1978, Turnbull won a <a href="/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship" title="Rhodes Scholarship">Rhodes Scholarship</a> and attended <a href="/wiki/Brasenose_College,_Oxford" title="Brasenose College, Oxford">Brasenose College, Oxford</a>, where he briefly studied for a business degree before switching to a <a href="/wiki/Postgraduate" class="mw-redirect" title="Postgraduate">postgraduate</a> <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Civil_Law" title="Bachelor of Civil Law">Bachelor of Civil Law</a> from 1978 to 1980, graduating with honours.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> While at Oxford, he worked for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sunday_Times" title="The Sunday Times">The Sunday Times</a></i> and contributed to newspapers and magazines in both the United States and Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> He befriended the future UK Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Theresa_May" title="Theresa May">Theresa May</a>, who credits Turnbull for encouraging her husband <a href="/wiki/Philip_May" title="Philip May">Sir Philip May</a> to propose to her.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull also met his future wife, <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Turnbull" title="Lucy Turnbull">Lucy</a>, during this time.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> During Turnbull's time at Oxford, a <a href="/wiki/Don_(honorific)#Academia" title="Don (honorific)">university don</a> wrote of him that he was "always going to enter life's rooms without knocking".<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Professional_career">Professional career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Costigan_commission">Costigan commission</span></h3>
<p>After graduating from Oxford, Turnbull returned to Australia and began working as a <a href="/wiki/Barrister" title="Barrister">barrister</a>. He was general counsel and secretary for <a href="/wiki/ACP_Magazines" class="mw-redirect" title="ACP Magazines">Australian Consolidated Press</a> Holdings Group from 1983 to 1985.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> During this time, he defended <a href="/wiki/Kerry_Packer" title="Kerry Packer">Kerry Packer</a> against the "Goanna" allegations made by the <a href="/wiki/Costigan_Commission#Broader_investigations" title="Costigan Commission">Costigan Commission</a>, accusing Packer of the murder of bank manager Ian Coote, among other crimes. The Commission believed that Packer had committed the murder to hide other criminal activities.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull attempted to use the press to goad the counsel assisting the commission, Douglas Meagher QC, into suing him and Packer for a 6000-word statement intentionally defamatory of Costigan and Meagher, accusing them of being "unjust, capricious, dishonest and malicious".<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The statement worked, and it was printed in full in many newspapers, increasing public opinion of Packer.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>  Turnbull later advised Packer to sue Meagher for defamation, an action that was struck down by Justice <a href="/wiki/David_Hunt_(judge)" title="David Hunt (judge)">David Hunt</a> as being an <a href="/wiki/Abuse_of_process" title="Abuse of process">abuse of process</a>, saying that Turnbull had managed "to poison the fountain of justice". These tactics made Turnbull enemies within the NSW Bar Association, leading to Turnbull's departure from that organisation.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> After the findings of the Costigan Commission were handed down, accusing Packer of tax evasion, drug trafficking and murder, Turnbull phoned <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Queensland" title="Premier of Queensland">Premier of Queensland</a> <a href="/wiki/Joh_Bjelke-Petersen" title="Joh Bjelke-Petersen">Joh Bjelke-Petersen</a>, requesting an inquest into the Commission's findings. Bjelke-Petersen agreed to hold the inquest, which refuted the Costigan Commission's allegations that Packer had committed murder.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Spycatcher_trial"><i>Spycatcher</i> trial</span></h3>
<p>In partnership with Bruce McWilliam, he established his own law firm, Turnbull McWilliam. During 1986, Turnbull defended <a href="/wiki/Peter_Wright_(MI5_officer)" title="Peter Wright (MI5 officer)">Peter Wright</a>, a former <a href="/wiki/MI5" title="MI5">MI5</a> official who wrote the book <i><a href="/wiki/Spycatcher" title="Spycatcher">Spycatcher</a>,</i> detailing his work for the spy agency during the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>. The <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">British</a> government had obtained an injunction to stop the book's sale, and wanted to do the same in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull argued that the book had nothing new in it, that other books with never-before-seen confidential information had been allowed by the British government with minimal changes while wanting to restrict a different book, and that the government had lied to the court through the trial.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>  The case was successful after going through the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Court_of_Appeal" title="New South Wales Court of Appeal">New South Wales Court of Appeal</a>, stopping the British government's attempts to suppress the book's publication in Australia. The case was widely reported, making Turnbull a public figure in Australia and the United Kingdom, and causing the book to sell over 2 million copies in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ABC_4Corners_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC_4Corners-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull later wrote a book on the trial, titled <i>The Spycatcher Trial</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>"The fact of the matter is that nothing is achieved in this world, particularly politically, other than with persistence, and persistence involves repetition and it involves argument and re-argument... The public interest in free speech is not just in truthful speech, in correct speech, in fair speech... The interest is in the debate. You see, every person who has ultimately changed the course of history has started off being unpopular." <i>Turnbull's closing submissions, 18 December 1986</i><sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_legal_work">Other legal work</span></h3>
<p>In 1987, Turnbull established an investment banking firm, Whitlam Turnbull &amp; Co Ltd, in partnership with <a href="/wiki/Neville_Wran" title="Neville Wran">Neville Wran</a>, the former <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_South_Wales" title="Premier of New South Wales">Premier of New South Wales</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Whitlam" title="Nicholas Whitlam">Nicholas Whitlam</a>, the former Chief Executive of the <a href="/wiki/State_Bank_of_New_South_Wales" title="State Bank of New South Wales">State Bank of New South Wales</a> and the son of former Labor Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a>. Whitlam parted company with the firm in 1990; it operated as Turnbull &amp; Partners Ltd until 1997.
</p><p>Turnbull left the firm he co-founded in 1997 to become a managing director of <a href="/wiki/Goldman_Sachs" title="Goldman Sachs">Goldman Sachs</a> Australia, eventually becoming a partner in Goldman Sachs and Co. Additionally, he worked as a director of Star Technology Systems from 1993 to 1995. During this time Turnbull was also the chairman of Axiom Forest Resources, which conducted logging in the <a href="/wiki/Solomon_Islands" title="Solomon Islands">Solomon Islands</a> under the trading name Silvania Forest Products. The latter's work was described by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_International_Development_Assistance_Bureau" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian International Development Assistance Bureau">Australian International Development Assistance Bureau</a> as a "<a href="/wiki/Clearcutting" title="Clearcutting">clear-felling</a> operation",<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> and the then Solomon Islands Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Solomon_Mamaloni" title="Solomon Mamaloni">Solomon Mamaloni</a> reportedly threatened to close it down for "constant breaches of logging practices", according to a critical article in the <i>Solomon Times</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turnbull purchased a stake in the <a href="/wiki/Internet_service_provider" title="Internet service provider">internet service provider</a> <a href="/wiki/OzEmail" title="OzEmail">OzEmail</a> in 1994 for $500,000. He sold this stake several months before the <a href="/wiki/Dot-com_bubble" title="Dot-com bubble">dot com bubble burst</a> in 1999 for $57&#160;million to then-telecommunications giant MCI <a href="/wiki/Worldcom" class="mw-redirect" title="Worldcom">Worldcom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In May 2002, Turnbull appeared before the <a href="/wiki/HIH_Insurance" title="HIH Insurance">HIH Insurance</a> <a href="/wiki/Royal_commission" title="Royal commission">royal commission</a> to be questioned on Goldman Sachs's involvement in the possible <a href="/wiki/Privatisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Privatisation">privatisation</a> of one of the acquisitions of the collapsed insurance company. The Royal Commissioner's report made no adverse findings against him or Goldman Sachs,<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> however, Turnbull was one of nine defendants who settled later litigation over the collapse in undisclosed payments, thought to be worth as much as $500m.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_involvement">Early political involvement</span></h2>
<p>In 1981, Turnbull stood for Liberal Party <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselection</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wentworth" title="Division of Wentworth">Division of Wentworth</a> prior to the <a href="/wiki/1981_Wentworth_by-election" title="1981 Wentworth by-election">1981 Wentworth by-election</a>. He was defeated by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Coleman" title="Peter Coleman">Peter Coleman</a>, who went on to win the seat.<sup id="cite_ref-ra_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ra-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In 1982, following his retirement from politics, former prime minister <a href="/wiki/William_McMahon" title="William McMahon">William McMahon</a> nominated Turnbull as his preferred successor in <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Lowe" title="Division of Lowe">Lowe</a>; the Liberals chose another candidate, and lost <a href="/wiki/1982_Lowe_by-election" title="1982 Lowe by-election">the by-election</a> to Labor.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull later attempted preselection in the safe state seat of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Mosman" title="Electoral district of Mosman">Mosman</a> in 1983, losing to <a href="/wiki/Phillip_Smiles" title="Phillip Smiles">Phillip Smiles</a>. He let his membership of the Liberal Party lapse in 1986, before rejoining in 2000.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull was made Federal Treasurer of the Liberal Party in 2000, and was a member of the party's federal and New South Wales executives from 2002 to 2003. He also spent time as a director of the <a href="/wiki/Menzies_Research_Centre" title="Menzies Research Centre">Menzies Research Centre</a>, the Liberal Party's research centre.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Australian_Republican_Movement">Australian Republican Movement</span></h3>
<p>In 1993, Turnbull was appointed by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Paul_Keating" title="Paul Keating">Paul Keating</a> as Chair of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_Advisory_Committee" title="Republic Advisory Committee">Republic Advisory Committee</a>, charged with exploring ways of moving Australia to a <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">republican form of government</a> by replacing the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_Australia" title="Monarchy of Australia">Queen of Australia</a> with an elected Australian head of state. Later that year, Turnbull became Chair of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Republic_Movement" title="Australian Republic Movement">Australian Republican Movement</a>, a position he would hold until 2000. He was an elected delegate at the <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_Constitutional_Convention" title="1998 Australian Constitutional Convention">1998 Australian Constitutional Convention</a> in <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> At the convention, Turnbull cautioned against mixing the roles of President and Prime Minister, advocating a <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_republic" title="Parliamentary republic">parliamentary republic</a>, and supported the <a href="/wiki/Bi-partisan_appointment_republican_model" title="Bi-partisan appointment republican model">bi-partisan appointment republican model</a> adopted by the convention.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turnbull was an active campaigner in the unsuccessful <a href="/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum" title="1999 Australian republic referendum">1999 referendum</a> to establish an <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">Australian republic</a>, serving as Chair of the Yes Committee. He published a book on the campaign, titled <i>Fighting for the Republic</i>. When the referendum failed, he accused incumbent Prime Minister and monarchist <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> of "breaking the nation's heart".<sup id="cite_ref-tguard_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tguard-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull retired from the Australian Republican Movement in 2000, having already left the board of <a href="/wiki/Ausflag" title="Ausflag">Ausflag</a> in 1994; he joined the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_Flag_Association" title="Australian National Flag Association">Australian National Flag Association</a> in 2004.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Choice_of_political_party">Choice of political party</span></h3>
<p>Turnbull has had a long affiliation with the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a> throughout his career. During his time in the Australian Republican Movement however, he considered running for preselection for the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a>. Turnbull's mother was close friends with <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_South_Wales" title="Premier of New South Wales">Premier of New South Wales</a> <a href="/wiki/Neville_Wran" title="Neville Wran">Neville Wran</a> and <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senator</a> <a href="/wiki/Lionel_Murphy" title="Lionel Murphy">Lionel Murphy</a>, who had briefly dated her in university. Both men were members of the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull himself was also friends with another Labor premier, <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> In 2015, it was revealed that Turnbull had held talks with Labor state politician <a href="/wiki/John_Della_Bosca" title="John Della Bosca">John Della Bosca</a> during the 1990s on a possible party switch, and that he had harboured aspirations in his youth to head the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Workers%27_Union" title="Australian Workers&#39; Union">Australian Workers' Union</a>, which is linked with the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The accusation, made by former Labor Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a>, was cited by Labor Leader <a href="/wiki/Bill_Shorten" title="Bill Shorten">Bill Shorten</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_Trade_Union_Governance_and_Corruption" title="Royal Commission into Trade Union Governance and Corruption">Royal Commission into Trade Union Governance and Corruption</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Howard_government">Howard government</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Entry_to_parliament">Entry to parliament</span></h3>
<p>In 2000, Turnbull intended to seek Liberal preselection for <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wentworth" title="Division of Wentworth">Wentworth</a> but did not eventually contest after concluding that preselection hopeful <a href="/wiki/Peter_King_(Australian_politician)" title="Peter King (Australian politician)">Peter King</a> had the numbers in the branches.<sup id="cite_ref-smhrise_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smhrise-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In 2003, Turnbull announced that he would challenge King for the seat and successfully defeated him to become the Liberal candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-smhrise_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smhrise-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> During what was a bitter preselection campaign, King accused Turnbull of <a href="/wiki/Branch_stacking" title="Branch stacking">branch stacking</a>, by having local members transferring their membership to a branch that would decide the pre-selection, what King referred to as "branch stripping".<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following his preselection loss, King stood against Turnbull at the <a href="/wiki/2004_Australian_federal_election" title="2004 Australian federal election">2004 federal election</a> as an <a href="/wiki/Independent_politician" title="Independent politician">independent</a> candidate. As a result, the traditionally safe Liberal seat became an electoral wildcard, the contest becoming a three-person race between Turnbull, King and the Labor candidate David Patch. During the campaign, Turnbull spent over <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>600,000 on his campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> While the Liberal primary vote eventually fell by 10.3% to a total 41.8%, King received only 18% of the primary vote with a 57%/43% Liberal/Labor preference split which meant Turnbull was elected, albeit on a reduced 55.5% <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party vote</a> after a 2.4% <a href="/wiki/Swing_(Australian_politics)" title="Swing (Australian politics)">swing</a>. The result meant that Wentworth was classified as a marginal seat for the first time since the <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 federal election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_Minister">Cabinet Minister</span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Howard_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Fourth Howard Ministry">Fourth Howard Ministry</a></div>
<p>Announcing a cabinet reshuffle on 24 January 2006, Prime Minister John Howard promoted Turnbull from the backbench to the role of <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_secretary" title="Parliamentary secretary">parliamentary secretary</a>, giving him special responsibility for water at the height of the <a href="/wiki/2000s_Australian_drought" title="2000s Australian drought">2000s Australian drought</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC-career_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC-career-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> On 26 September 2006, Howard announced the creation of a new Office of Water Resources, sitting within the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_the_Prime_Minister_and_Cabinet_(Australia)" title="Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet (Australia)">Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Turnbull_aph_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Turnbull_aph-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> to address the problem of drought in Australia; Turnbull was given responsibility for this office.
</p><p>In January 2007, Howard promoted Turnbull to the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">Cabinet</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_the_Environment_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of the Environment (Australia)">Minister for the Environment and Water</a>. In this position, Turnbull approved a proposed <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>1.7&#160;billion <a href="/wiki/Bell_Bay_Pulp_Mill" title="Bell Bay Pulp Mill">Bell Bay Pulp Mill</a> in north <a href="/wiki/Tasmania" title="Tasmania">Tasmania</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Launceston,_Tasmania" title="Launceston, Tasmania">Launceston</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-AGE_approves_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AGE_approves-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull's approval of the Bell Bay Pulp Mill project of <a href="/wiki/Gunns" title="Gunns">Gunns</a> came on 4 October 2007 and followed a report by the government's chief scientist <a href="/wiki/Jim_Peacock" title="Jim Peacock">Jim Peacock</a> on the project's potential environmental impact, which required the project to meet 48 "strict environmental" conditions.
</p><p>In February 2007, Turnbull was criticised for claiming a government allowance of <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>175 a night and paying it to his wife as rent while living in a townhouse owned by her in Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 federal election</a> campaign, Turnbull announced that if re-elected the government would contribute <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>10&#160;million to the investigation of an untried Russian technology that aims to trigger rainfall from the atmosphere, even when there are no clouds. The Australian Rain Corporation presented research documents written in Russian, explained by a Russian researcher who spoke to local experts in Russian.<sup id="cite_ref-rainmaking_gamble_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rainmaking_gamble-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Although Turnbull claimed that the Australian Rain Corporation was Australian-based, investigations revealed that it was 75% Swiss-owned. It was also revealed that a prominent stakeholder in the Australian Rain Corporation, Matt Handbury, is a nephew of <a href="/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch" title="Rupert Murdoch">Rupert Murdoch</a>. Turnbull refused to answer questions regarding Handbury's contribution to the Wentworth Forum, the main fund-raising organisation for Turnbull's 2007 election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-rainmaking_gamble_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rainmaking_gamble-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition">Opposition</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Aftermath_of_2007_election">Aftermath of 2007 election</span></h3>
<p>Turnbull retained his seat at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 federal election</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party vote</a> 1.3% <a href="/wiki/Swing_(Australian_politics)" title="Swing (Australian politics)">swing</a> in Wentworth, despite a 5.6% swing away from the Coalition in the state, and a 5.4% swing against them nationwide.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> After John Howard lost his seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Bennelong" title="Division of Bennelong">Bennelong</a>, on 25 November 2007 <a href="/wiki/Peter_Costello" title="Peter Costello">Peter Costello</a>, who Howard stated publicly should succeed him, announced he would not seek the party leadership. Turnbull declared his candidacy later that same day, and was considered by the media as a favourite.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 29 November he <a href="/wiki/2007_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_election" title="2007 Liberal Party of Australia leadership election">narrowly lost</a> the leadership vote to <a href="/wiki/Brendan_Nelson" title="Brendan Nelson">Brendan Nelson</a> by three votes; Nelson quickly appointed Turnbull <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow Cabinet">Shadow Treasurer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Nelson_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nelson-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after the vote, fellow Shadow Cabinet Minister <a href="/wiki/Nick_Minchin" title="Nick Minchin">Nick Minchin</a> publicly suggested that Turnbull's failure to consult with party colleagues before declaring his opinion to the media on issues such as an apology to the <a href="/wiki/Stolen_Generations" title="Stolen Generations">Stolen Generations</a> was what had cost him the leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> This led to a disagreement between the two and culminated in Minchin privately telling Turnbull that he was "too fucking sensitive."<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In May 2008, Turnbull led the Coalition response to the <a href="/wiki/2008_Australian_federal_budget" title="2008 Australian federal budget">2008 Australian federal budget</a>, criticising the increased taxes on luxury cars and certain alcoholic drinks, citing a possible increase in inflation as a concern.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_.282008.E2.80.932009.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_(2008–2009)">Leader of the Opposition (2008–2009)</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Centralcoastpresser.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Centralcoastpresser.jpg/220px-Centralcoastpresser.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Centralcoastpresser.jpg/330px-Centralcoastpresser.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Centralcoastpresser.jpg/440px-Centralcoastpresser.jpg 2x" data-file-width="528" data-file-height="345" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull with Deputy Leader <a href="/wiki/Julie_Bishop" title="Julie Bishop">Julie Bishop</a> (right) and <a href="/wiki/Helen_Coonan" title="Helen Coonan">Helen Coonan</a> (left) in July 2009</figcaption></figure>
<p>After months of consistently poor opinion polling, Turnbull <a href="/wiki/2008_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="2008 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">challenged</a> <a href="/wiki/Brendan_Nelson" title="Brendan Nelson">Brendan Nelson</a> for the leadership on 16 September 2008. He won the ballot by four votes and became <a href="/wiki/List_of_Australian_Leaders_of_the_Opposition" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Australian Leaders of the Opposition">Leader of the Opposition</a>. Later that month, Turnbull confessed that he had smoked <a href="/wiki/Cannabis_(drug)" title="Cannabis (drug)">marijuana</a> in his younger days, becoming the first Liberal Leader to make such an admission.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In early 2009, Turnbull appointed <a href="/wiki/Chris_Kenny" title="Chris Kenny">Chris Kenny</a>, a former staffer to <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Downer" title="Alexander Downer">Alexander Downer</a> and an <i><a href="/wiki/The_Advertiser_(Adelaide)" title="The Advertiser (Adelaide)">Advertiser</a></i> journalist, as his chief of staff.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> In May 2009, Turnbull attacked the <a href="/wiki/2009_Australian_federal_budget" title="2009 Australian federal budget">2009 Australian federal budget</a> which came amidst the fallout from the <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932008" class="mw-redirect" title="Financial crisis of 2007–2008">global financial crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2009, Godwin Grech, a <a href="/wiki/Department_of_the_Treasury_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of the Treasury (Australia)">Treasury</a> civil servant, privately contacted Turnbull, alleging that a car dealer with links to the Labor Party had received preferential treatment under the OzCar program, sparking the '<a href="/wiki/Utegate" title="Utegate">OzCar affair</a>'. Turnbull later repeated these allegations in Parliament, stating that Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> and <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a> <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Swan" title="Wayne Swan">Wayne Swan</a> had "used their offices and taxpayers' resources to seek advantage for one of their mates and then lied about it to the Parliament" and that they needed to "either explain their actions or resign".<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> On 22 June, the email Grech had secretly provided to Turnbull supporting allegation was alleged to have been faked by Grech. Grech subsequently admitted the forgery, with an <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_Audit_Office" title="Australian National Audit Office">Australian National Audit Office</a> inquiry on 4 August clearing both Rudd and Swan of any wrongdoing.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Rudd_Swan_cleared_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rudd_Swan_cleared-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The resulting embarrassment of having repeated false allegations, as well as Turnbull's demeanour throughout the OzCar affair, was judged as the cause of a subsequent significant decline in his approval ratings in opinion polls.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 24 November 2009, Liberal and National MPs and <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senators</a> met to discuss the Rudd government's proposed <a href="/wiki/Carbon_Pollution_Reduction_Scheme" title="Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme">Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme</a> (CPRS). Turnbull announced that his policy would be to support the CPRS, despite significant disagreement among his colleagues.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> In response, Liberal MPs <a href="/wiki/Wilson_Tuckey" title="Wilson Tuckey">Wilson Tuckey</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Jensen" title="Dennis Jensen">Dennis Jensen</a> looked to move a leadership spill motion, intending to nominate <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Andrews_(politician)" title="Kevin Andrews (politician)">Kevin Andrews</a> as a challenger to Turnbull.<sup id="cite_ref-three_quit_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-three_quit-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> While this attempt failed, increasing numbers of MPs and Senators publicly criticised the position, with several resigning from the Shadow Cabinet, including <a href="/wiki/Tony_Abbott" title="Tony Abbott">Tony Abbott</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-desert_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-desert-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 1 December 2009, just one week after Turnbull announced the policy on the CPRS, Abbott announced he would <a href="/wiki/2009_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="2009 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">challenge</a> Turnbull for the leadership. Though initially regarded as having little chance of success, with Turnbull stating in public that Abbott did not have the numbers to win, Abbott defeated Turnbull in the ballot by a single vote.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> After the shock result, Turnbull returned to the backbench and said he would serve out the remainder of his term as Member for Wentworth.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> On 6 April 2010, he announced he would not seek re-election to the Australian Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> However, on 1 May 2010 he reversed this decision saying that he had been convinced by former Prime Minister John Howard to not give up his political career.<sup id="cite_ref-reversal_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-reversal-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-tguard_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tguard-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Shadow_Minister_.282010.E2.80.932013.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Shadow_Minister_(2010–2013)">Shadow Minister (2010–2013)</span></h3>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/2010_Australian_federal_election" title="2010 Australian federal election">2010 federal election</a>, Turnbull was re-elected with an 11.01% <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party</a> <a href="/wiki/Swing_(Australian_politics)" title="Swing (Australian politics)">swing</a> towards him.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> After discussing the possibility of a return to the Shadow Cabinet with Tony Abbott, Turnbull was made <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Communications_(Australia)" title="Minister for Communications (Australia)">Shadow Minister for Communications</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> In his first policy announcement in the role, Turnbull stated that a Coalition government would "demolish" the recently introduced <a href="/wiki/National_Broadband_Network" title="National Broadband Network">National Broadband Network</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Delivering the 2012 Alfred Deakin Lecture on digital liberty, he spoke out strongly against the Gillard government's proposed two-year data retention law.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on a self-published source. (December 2014)">self-published source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In July 2012, Turnbull was criticised by some Liberal MPs for saying that civil unions should be introduced as a first step towards establishing <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Australia" title="Same-sex marriage in Australia">same-sex marriage in Australia</a>. Tony Abbott rejected Turnbull's suggestion of holding a conscience vote on the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Abbott_government">Abbott government</span></h2>
<h3><span id="Minister_for_Communications_.282013.E2.80.932015.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_for_Communications_(2013–2015)">Minister for Communications (2013–2015)</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MalcolmTurnbull.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/MalcolmTurnbull.jpg/170px-MalcolmTurnbull.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/MalcolmTurnbull.jpg/255px-MalcolmTurnbull.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/MalcolmTurnbull.jpg/340px-MalcolmTurnbull.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2502" data-file-height="3508" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull at the 2014 <a href="/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union" title="International Telecommunication Union">International Telecommunication Union</a> Conference in South Korea</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 9 April 2013, Turnbull and Tony Abbott presented their party's alternative <a href="/wiki/National_Broadband_Network" title="National Broadband Network">National Broadband Network</a> (NBN) plan.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> The plan prioritised a modified and scaled-down NBN with "<a href="/wiki/Fiber_to_the_x" title="Fiber to the x">fibre to the node</a>" (FTTN) and <a href="/wiki/Last_mile_(telecommunications)" title="Last mile (telecommunications)">last-mile</a> by <a href="/wiki/Copper_conductor" title="Copper conductor">copper cable</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kohler2013-03-10_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kohler2013-03-10-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> The new policy contrasted with the previous position which had called for the dismantling of the entire NBN.<sup id="cite_ref-Kohler2013-03-10_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kohler2013-03-10-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the Coalition victory in the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 federal election</a>, Turnbull was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Communications_(Australia)" title="Minister for Communications (Australia)">Minister for Communications</a> and began implementing the alternative NBN strategy. In 2014, Turnbull announced that the Vertigan Report, a <a href="/wiki/Cost%E2%80%93benefit_analysis" title="Cost–benefit analysis">cost–benefit analysis</a> of providing fast broadband to regional and rural Australia through wireless and satellite services, revealed that continuing the plan would cost nearly <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>5&#160;billion and was expected to produce only <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>600&#160;million in economic benefits – a return of just 10%. In spite of the economic cost, Turnbull stated that whilst subsidising broadband to regional areas is "fiendishly expensive", there was no other option.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2014, Turnbull brokered a deal between the Australian government, <a href="/wiki/NBN_Co" title="NBN Co">NBN Co</a> and <a href="/wiki/Telstra" title="Telstra">Telstra</a> whereby NBN Co acquired Telstra's copper network and hybrid-fibre coaxial (HFC) to deliver the NBN. Telstra and NBN Co agreed to work together on the FTTN trial involving 200,000 premises.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> In August 2015, Turnbull revealed that the overall end cost of the network build would likely expand up to an additional $15&#160;billion, with NBN Co likely to take on the additional expenditure as debt. Though still cheaper than the original Labor Party NBN policy, which aimed to deliver much faster connection speeds, the peak funding requirement under the Liberal model ran to between $46&#160;billion and $56&#160;billion.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="February_2015_leadership_spill_motion">February 2015 leadership spill motion</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/February_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill_motion" title="February 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill motion">February 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill motion</a></div>
<p>Following persistent leadership tensions amidst poor opinion polling, a <a href="/wiki/February_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill_motion" title="February 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill motion">leadership spill motion</a> was moved against Tony Abbott on 9 February 2015. Although the spill motion was defeated 61 votes to 39, Turnbull had been reported as considering a run for the leadership if the spill motion had succeeded. Before the motion Turnbull had told reporters that "if, for whatever reason, the leadership of a political party is vacant then anyone, any member of the party can stand, whether they be a minister or a backbencher, without any disloyalty to the person whose leadership has been declared vacant."<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_of_Australia_.282015.E2.80.932018.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_of_Australia_(2015–2018)">Prime Minister of Australia (2015–2018)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Turnbull_government" title="Turnbull government">Turnbull government</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/First_Turnbull_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Turnbull Ministry">First Turnbull Ministry</a> and <a href="/wiki/Second_Turnbull_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Turnbull Ministry">Second Turnbull Ministry</a></div>
