<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="September_2015_leadership_election">September 2015 leadership election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/September_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="September 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">September 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg/220px-Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg/330px-Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg/440px-Malcolm_Turnbull_swearing-in_ceremony_September_2015.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1363" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull sworn in as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">Prime Minister</a> by <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> Sir <a href="/wiki/Peter_Cosgrove" title="Peter Cosgrove">Peter Cosgrove</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite the defeat of the February 2015 spill motion, questions over Abbott's leadership did not abate, with the government consistently performing poorly in opinion polls. On 14 September 2015, after 30 consecutive Newspolls had put the Liberals far behind Labor, Turnbull resigned from the Cabinet and announced he would challenge Abbott for the leadership of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull stated that Abbott "was not&#160;capable of providing&#160;the economic leadership we need" and that the Liberal Party needs a "style of&#160;leadership that respects the&#160;people's intelligence."<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull defeated Abbott by 54 votes to 44 at the <a href="/wiki/September_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="September 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">subsequent leadership ballot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> He was sworn in as the 29th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a> the following day.<sup id="cite_ref-swornin_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-swornin-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TakingOffice_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TakingOffice-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turnbull announced an extensive reshuffle of the Cabinet on 20 September 2015 to form the <a href="/wiki/First_Turnbull_ministry" title="First Turnbull ministry">first Turnbull ministry</a>. Notably, he increased the number of female Cabinet ministers from two to five and appointed <a href="/wiki/Marise_Payne" title="Marise Payne">Marise Payne</a> as Australia's first female <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for Defence</a>. The number of Cabinet ministers rose from 19 to 21. On Turnbull's key policy differences with Abbott, particularly <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Australia" title="Climate change in Australia">climate change</a>, republicanism and <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Australia" title="Same-sex marriage in Australia">same-sex marriage</a>, he stated that there would be no immediate change before any election.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/National_Party_of_Australia" title="National Party of Australia">Nationals</a> successfully negotiated a total of $4&#160;billion worth of deals from Turnbull, as well as control of the water portfolio, in exchange for a continued <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Coalition</a> agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull stated that he would not lead a government that did not take climate change seriously.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2016_federal_election">2016 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_election" title="2016 Australian federal election">2016 Australian federal election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg/220px-Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg/330px-Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg/440px-Malcolm_Turnbull_visits_Peter_Cosgrove_to_request_double_dissolution.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="427" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull visits Peter Cosgrove to request both Houses of Parliament be dissolved ahead of a double dissolution election.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 21 March 2016, Turnbull announced that Parliament would consider bills to reinstate the <a href="/wiki/Office_of_the_Australian_Building_and_Construction_Commissioner" title="Office of the Australian Building and Construction Commissioner">Australian Building and Construction Commission</a> (ABCC), with the bills having previously been rejected twice before. Turnbull stated if the Senate rejected the bills a third time, he would advise the governor-general, <a href="/wiki/Sir_Peter_Cosgrove" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Peter Cosgrove">Sir Peter Cosgrove</a>, to call a <a href="/wiki/Double_dissolution" title="Double dissolution">double dissolution</a> of Parliament and a federal election for 2 July. Turnbull also brought forward the delivery of the <a href="/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_budget" title="2016 Australian federal budget">federal budget</a> from 10 to 3 May to facilitate this.<sup id="cite_ref-aprilrecall_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aprilrecall-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> On 18 April, the Senate once again rejected the bills to reinstate the ABCC. On 8 May, Turnbull visited <a href="/wiki/Government_House,_Canberra" title="Government House, Canberra">Government House</a> to advise Cosgrove to issue the writs for a double dissolution on 9 May; this confirmed the date of the election as 2 July 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_election" title="2016 Australian federal election">2016 federal election</a> campaign, a ReachTEL opinion poll of 626 <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Wentworth" title="Division of Wentworth">Wentworth</a> voters conducted on 31 May predicted a <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party</a> <a href="/wiki/Swing_(Australian_politics)" title="Swing (Australian politics)">swing</a> against Turnbull for the first time since his election to Wentworth, revealing a reduced 58% two-party vote from a large 10.9% two-party swing.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> A controversy occurred during the election campaign, when the president of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_Imams_Council" title="Australian National Imams Council">Australian National Imams Council</a>, Sheikh <a href="/wiki/Shady_Alsuleiman" title="Shady Alsuleiman">Shady Alsuleiman</a> participated in an <a href="/wiki/Iftar" title="Iftar">Iftar</a> dinner hosted by Turnbull at <a href="/wiki/Kirribilli_House" title="Kirribilli House">Kirribilli House</a>. Turnbull said he would not have invited Alsuleiman if he had known of his position regarding homosexuals.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the election, the Coalition lost 14 seats and retained majority government by a single seat.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> The result was the closest since the <a href="/wiki/1961_Australian_federal_election" title="1961 Australian federal election">1961 federal election</a>. In the days following the election, when the result was still not certain, Turnbull had to negotiate with the <a href="/wiki/Crossbencher" title="Crossbencher">crossbench</a> to secure <a href="/wiki/Confidence_and_supply" title="Confidence and supply">confidence and supply</a> support from <a href="/wiki/Bob_Katter" title="Bob Katter">Bob Katter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Wilkie" title="Andrew Wilkie">Andrew Wilkie</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cathy_McGowan_(politician)" title="Cathy McGowan (politician)">Cathy McGowan</a> in the event of a <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a> and resulting <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-lead1_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lead1-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2017, Turnbull confirmed he had donated $1.75&#160;million to the Liberal Party's election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Asylum_seeker_policy">Asylum seeker policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government#Asylum_seekers" title="Abbott government">Abbott government §&#160;Asylum seekers</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government#Asylum_seekers" title="Gillard government">Gillard government §&#160;Asylum seekers</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg/220px-Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg/330px-Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg/440px-Donald_Trump_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_2017.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="748" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull and U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a> in New York City, May 2017</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Asylum_in_Australia" title="Asylum in Australia">Asylum seeker policy</a> is a contentious <a href="/wiki/Wedge_issue" title="Wedge issue">wedge issue</a> in Australian politics, especially since the <a href="/wiki/Tampa_affair" title="Tampa affair">Tampa affair</a>. Continuing the bipartisan stance of <a href="/wiki/Operation_Sovereign_Borders" title="Operation Sovereign Borders">Operation Sovereign Borders</a> has been at the forefront the Coalition's <a href="/wiki/Asylum_seeker" title="Asylum seeker">asylum seeker</a> policy. Around 1,250 asylum seekers remain in the offshore processing centres on <a href="/wiki/Manus_Island" title="Manus Island">Manus Island</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nauru" title="Nauru">Nauru</a>. In August 2016, protestors called for the closure of camps on Manus and Nauru<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> after <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a></i> released leaked incident reports alleging "routine dysfunction and cruelty" on Nauru.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 2016, the <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama" title="Presidency of Barack Obama">Obama administration</a> set up a refugee center in <a href="/wiki/Costa_Rica" title="Costa Rica">Costa Rica</a> in response to a Central American <a href="/wiki/2014_American_immigration_crisis" title="2014 American immigration crisis">migration crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> In November, Turnbull and <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> announced that Australia would accept 1,250 refugees from Central America, in exchange for the U.S. accepting refugees on Nauru and Manus.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turnbull and President <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a> held a phone conversation on 28 January 2017, the transcript of which was later leaked to <i><a href="/wiki/The_Washington_Post" title="The Washington Post">The Washington Post</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> On 2 February 2017, Trump tweeted that Obama's deal was "dumb".<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> US Vice President <a href="/wiki/Mike_Pence" title="Mike Pence">Mike Pence</a> later confirmed that the United States would honour the deal, subject to "extreme vetting" of asylum seekers.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Australia began receiving Central American asylum seekers in July 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Energy_policy">Energy policy</span></h3>
<p>Since the 2016 election, the Turnbull government had followed prior Coalition government energy policies. This involved the wholesale dismissal of <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_renewable_energy_target" title="Mandatory renewable energy target">renewable energy targets</a> and <a href="/wiki/Emissions_trading" title="Emissions trading">emissions intensity schemes</a>. This had only hardened when <a href="/wiki/South_Australia" title="South Australia">South Australia</a> faced large blackouts, which Turnbull had blamed on the state's "ambitious" renewable energy target.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> In response to the gas and energy crisis that occurred in March 2017,<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Malcolm Turnbull announced a 50% increase in the capacity of <a href="/wiki/Snowy_Hydro" title="Snowy Hydro">Snowy Hydro</a> through "pumped hydro" technology.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2017, Turnbull announced that he would use the Commonwealth government's powers to place export restrictions on the nation's <a href="/wiki/Liquefied_natural_gas" title="Liquefied natural gas">liquified natural gas</a> ("LNG") industry.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> He announced that these changes were in response to the high wholesale gas prices that were a result of a shortage of gas in the domestic gas market, and that it was "unacceptable" that domestic prices were so high, indicating that a consequence of these restrictions would be a decrease in the wholesale gas price. The <a href="/wiki/Multinational_corporation" title="Multinational corporation">multinational</a> gas companies and the gas industry association heavily criticised the policy, saying that it would neither increase supply nor reduce the wholesale price of gas.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg/220px-Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg/330px-Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg/440px-Turnbull_selfie_with_Xi_Trump_Quang.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1365" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull takes a selfie with <a href="/wiki/Tr%E1%BA%A7n_%C4%90%E1%BA%A1i_Quang" title="Trần Đại Quang">Trần Đại Quang</a>, <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Xi_Jinping" title="Xi Jinping">Xi Jinping</a>, November 2017</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Same-sex_marriage_plebiscite">Same-sex marriage plebiscite</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Marriage_Law_Postal_Survey" title="Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey">Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey</a> and <a href="/wiki/Marriage_Amendment_(Definition_and_Religious_Freedoms)_Act_2017" title="Marriage Amendment (Definition and Religious Freedoms) Act 2017">Marriage Amendment (Definition and Religious Freedoms) Act 2017</a></div>
<p>Prior to Turnbull becoming Prime Minister, the parliamentary Liberal Party voted to resolve the issue of same-sex marriage by putting the question to Australians voters via a <a href="/wiki/Referendum" title="Referendum">plebiscite</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Enabling legislation was rejected twice by the Senate,<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> and so the government decided to adopt a <a href="/wiki/Australian_Marriage_Law_Postal_Survey" title="Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey">postal plebiscite option</a>, which involved the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Bureau_of_Statistics" title="Australian Bureau of Statistics">Australian Bureau of Statistics</a> conducting a nationwide survey asking voters whether they would like to see a change in the definition of marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Sending out of ballots began on 12 September 2017, as attempts to prevent the survey through a High Court challenge failed. The survey ended 7 November 2017 and results released 15 November the same year. It returned with a total of 7,817,247 (61.6%) "Yes" responses and 4,873,987 (38.4%) "No" responses.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the vote, after four days of debates regarding amendments which included proposals to increase religious protections to refuse services to same-sex couples, on 7 December 2017 same-sex marriage was legalised through a parliamentary vote by the House of Representatives; Turnbull himself voted "Yes".<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> The first same-sex marriages in Australia occurred as a result of the law change from 9 January 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Parliamentary_eligibility_crisis">Parliamentary eligibility crisis</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_(34664387820).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_%2834664387820%29.jpg/220px-CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_%2834664387820%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_%2834664387820%29.jpg/330px-CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_%2834664387820%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_%2834664387820%29.jpg/440px-CJCS_Attends_Shangri-La_Dialogue_PB383_%2834664387820%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4928" data-file-height="3280" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull in June 2017</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_Australian_parliamentary_eligibility_crisis" title="2017–18 Australian parliamentary eligibility crisis">2017–18 Australian parliamentary eligibility crisis</a></div>
<p>Members of Turnbull's government were among those embroiled in the parliamentary eligibility crisis that arose in 2017, which disqualified several parliamentarians who held dual citizenship in accordance with <a href="/wiki/Section_44_of_the_Constitution_of_Australia" title="Section 44 of the Constitution of Australia">subsection 44(i) of the Australian Constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Three Cabinet members were among the "Citizenship Seven" whose cases were heard in the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court of Australia</a>: the leader and deputy leader of the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">co-governing</a> <a href="/wiki/National_Party_of_Australia" title="National Party of Australia">National Party</a>, Deputy Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Barnaby_Joyce" title="Barnaby Joyce">Barnaby Joyce</a>, Senator <a href="/wiki/Fiona_Nash" title="Fiona Nash">Fiona Nash</a>, and Resources Minister <a href="/wiki/Matt_Canavan" title="Matt Canavan">Matt Canavan</a>, who resigned from Cabinet after discovering his potential dual citizenship.<sup id="cite_ref-section44_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-section44-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> The High Court <a href="/wiki/Re_Canavan" title="Re Canavan">ruled</a> that Canavan was eligible, but disqualified dual citizens Joyce and Nash from Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-high_court_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-high_court-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-abc_10_2017_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc_10_2017-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-smh_10_2017_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smh_10_2017-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto4_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto4-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Turnbull government temporarily lost its one-seat majority in the House of Representatives after Joyce's disqualification and the resignation of Liberal Party MP <a href="/wiki/John_Alexander_(Australian_politician)" title="John Alexander (Australian politician)">John Alexander</a>, who also held dual citizenship. However, in December 2017 both Joyce and Alexander, having renounced their foreign citizenships, contested and won <a href="/wiki/List_of_Australian_federal_by-elections" title="List of Australian federal by-elections">by-elections</a> in their former seats of <a href="/wiki/2017_New_England_by-election" title="2017 New England by-election">New England</a> and <a href="/wiki/2017_Bennelong_by-election" title="2017 Bennelong by-election">Bennelong</a> respectively, thereby retaining Turnbull's governing majority in the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="August_2018_leadership_spills">August 2018 leadership spills</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2018_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spills" title="2018 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills">2018 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills</a></div>
<p>On 21 August 2018, Turnbull survived a challenge to his leadership of the Liberal Party by Home Affairs Minister <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a>, winning by 48 votes to 35.<sup id="cite_ref-abc_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-guardian_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Leadership_spill" title="Leadership spill">spill</a> highlighted ideological tensions within the Liberal Party, between the <a href="/wiki/Moderates_(Liberal_Party_of_Australia)" title="Moderates (Liberal Party of Australia)">moderate wing</a> led by Turnbull and the conservative wing represented by Dutton and Tony Abbott. From 21 to 23 August, tensions mounted and Dutton announced that he would seek a second spill. Turnbull responded that, pending a report from the <a href="/wiki/Solicitor-General_of_Australia" title="Solicitor-General of Australia">Solicitor-General of Australia</a> on the eligibility of Dutton to serve in Parliament and the receipt of a petition calling for a party room meeting that bore the signatures of at least half (43) of the parliamentary party, he would call such a meeting, vacate the leadership (regarding the petition as a vote of no confidence) and not stand in the subsequent leadership election. On the morning of 24 August, the Solicitor-General advised that Peter Dutton was "not ineligible" to serve. Later that morning, Dutton presented to Turnbull a document calling for a party room meeting that contained the minimum 43 signatures.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
A party meeting was then called and the leadership was spilled, with <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison" title="Scott Morrison">Scott Morrison</a> elected as Turnbull's successor by 45 votes over Dutton with 40. In his final press conference as Prime Minister, Turnbull denounced Dutton and Abbott as "wreckers".<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 27 August Turnbull announced that he would resign from Parliament over the coming days. On 31 August 2018 he tendered a formal notice of resignation to the <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the Australian House of Representatives">Speaker of the House of Representatives</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_ideology">Political ideology</span></h2>
<p>Coming from the <a href="/wiki/Moderates_(Liberal_Party_of_Australia)" title="Moderates (Liberal Party of Australia)">moderate</a> faction of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party</a>, Turnbull has been described as being pragmatic and holding a <a href="/wiki/Centrism" title="Centrism">centrist</a> ideology,<sup id="cite_ref-TheConvo_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TheConvo-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> with <a href="/wiki/Progressivism" title="Progressivism">progressive</a><sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Social_liberalism" title="Social liberalism">socially liberal</a> views.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> An acolyte of former <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">Prime Ministers</a> <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Deakin" title="Alfred Deakin">Alfred Deakin</a><sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-DisraliSpeech_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DisraliSpeech-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TheConvo_177-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TheConvo-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull said in a 2017 speech: "In 1944 Menzies went to great pains not to call his new political party, consolidating the centre right of Australian politics, "conservative" – but rather the Liberal Party, which he firmly anchored in the centre of Australian politics." Later adding: "The sensible centre, to use my predecessor Tony Abbott's phrase, was the place to be and it remains the place to be now."<sup id="cite_ref-DisraliSpeech_183-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DisraliSpeech-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life_after_politics">Life after politics</span></h2>
<p>On 1 June 2019, Turnbull returned to the private sector as a senior advisor to major global private equity firm <a href="/wiki/KKR" class="mw-redirect" title="KKR">KKR</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull returned to Australia in December 2019 and appeared on the final episode of <i><a href="/wiki/Q%26A_(Australian_talk_show)" class="mw-redirect" title="Q&amp;A (Australian talk show)">Q&amp;A</a></i> hosted by Tony Jones on 9 December 2019.
</p><p>Turnbull has publicly criticised the <a href="/wiki/Morrison_government" title="Morrison government">Morrison government</a> for not taking strong enough action on climate change, arguing that they should readopt his <a href="/wiki/National_Energy_Guarantee" title="National Energy Guarantee">National Energy Guarantee</a> (NEG) policy. In a piece in <i>The Guardian</i>, he stated that, "Scott Morrison can't afford to waste the bushfire crisis when Australia urgently needs its own <a href="/wiki/Green_New_Deal" title="Green New Deal">Green New Deal</a>...There are simply no more excuses. We cannot allow political prejudice and vested interests to hold us up any longer. If ever there was a crisis not to waste, it is this one. Morrison has the chance now to reinstate the NEG with higher targets. Both he and Josh Frydenberg were among its strongest supporters when I was PM. They abandoned it in the lead-up to an election, to pacify the right wing of the Coalition that sabotaged it in the first place."<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 25 October 2020, Turnbull gave his support to former Australian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a>'s petition for a "Royal Commission to ensure a strong, diverse Australian news media" with the goal of investigating <a href="/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch" title="Rupert Murdoch">Rupert Murdoch</a>'s control over Australian news media, tweeting that he had signed it and encouraging others to follow suit.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> The petition became the most signed parliamentary e-petition in Australia, with more than 500,000 signatures.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> The petition was tabled in the House of Representatives by <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> MP <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Leigh" title="Andrew Leigh">Andrew Leigh</a> on 9 November 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2021, Turnbull joined the board of the <a href="/wiki/International_Hydropower_Association" title="International Hydropower Association">International Hydropower Association</a> as a non-executive member, also becoming a co-chair of the organisation's International Forum on Pumped Storage Hydropower.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2023, Turnbull gave a speech at the Centre for Asia Pacific Resilience and Innovation, at the organisation's annual forum in <a href="/wiki/Taipei" title="Taipei">Taipei</a>, <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a>. In the speech, he stated that Taiwan was under a greater threat from local actors who spread lies than external forces, seemingly hinting at recent issues involving China.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_(6707565323).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_%286707565323%29.jpg/170px-Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_%286707565323%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="229" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_%286707565323%29.jpg/255px-Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_%286707565323%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_%286707565323%29.jpg/340px-Lucy_and_Malcolm_Turnbull_%286707565323%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="963" data-file-height="1295" /></a><figcaption>Turnbull and his wife <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Turnbull" title="Lucy Turnbull">Lucy Turnbull</a>, 2003–04 <a href="/wiki/List_of_mayors,_lord_mayors_and_administrators_of_Sydney" title="List of mayors, lord mayors and administrators of Sydney">Sydney Lord Mayor</a>, in January 2012</figcaption></figure>
<p>Turnbull is married to <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Turnbull" title="Lucy Turnbull">Lucy Turnbull</a> (<span title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names" title="Birth name">née</a>&#160;Hughes</span>), who was the <a href="/wiki/List_of_mayors,_lord_mayors_and_administrators_of_Sydney" title="List of mayors, lord mayors and administrators of Sydney">Lord Mayor of Sydney</a> from 2003 to 2004 and has held a number of other prominent positions. The couple were married on 22 March 1980 at <a href="/wiki/Cumnor" title="Cumnor">Cumnor</a>, Oxfordshire, by a <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> priest while Turnbull was attending the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hawkins_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hawkins-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> They live in the eastern suburbs of Sydney.<sup id="cite_ref-AboutMalcolm_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AboutMalcolm-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turnbull and Lucy have two adult children, Alex and Daisy, and as of July 2016<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Malcolm_Turnbull&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, three grandchildren.<sup id="cite_ref-AboutMalcolm_193-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AboutMalcolm-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Alex Turnbull is married to Yvonne Wang, who is of Chinese descent.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The use of Bligh as a male middle name is a tradition in the Turnbull family. It is also Turnbull's son's middle name. One of Turnbull's ancestors was colonist John Turnbull, who named his youngest son William Bligh Turnbull in honour of deposed <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_New_South_Wales" title="Governor of New South Wales">Governor</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Bligh" title="William Bligh">William Bligh</a> at the time of the <a href="/wiki/Rum_Rebellion" title="Rum Rebellion">Rum Rebellion</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2008, Turnbull became the first Liberal leader to have admitted to smoking <a href="/wiki/Cannabis" title="Cannabis">cannabis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<p>Raised <a href="/wiki/Presbyterianism" title="Presbyterianism">Presbyterian</a>, Turnbull became agnostic in the beginning of his adult life and later converted to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Roman Catholicism</a> "by mid-2002"; his wife's family is Roman Catholic.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> However, he has found himself at odds with the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_abortion" title="Catholic Church and abortion">church's teaching on abortion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stem_cell_controversy#Catholicism" title="Stem cell controversy">stem cell research</a> and <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_homosexuality" title="Catholic Church and homosexuality">same-sex marriage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull supported legislation relaxing restrictions on abortion pill <a href="/wiki/Mifepristone" title="Mifepristone">RU486</a>, and he also voted for the legalisation of <a href="/wiki/Somatic_cell_nuclear_transfer" title="Somatic cell nuclear transfer">somatic cell nuclear transfer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> He did so despite vocal opposition to both proposals by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/George_Pell" title="George Pell">George Pell</a>, the then-<a href="/wiki/Catholic_Bishops_and_Archbishops_of_Sydney" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Bishops and Archbishops of Sydney">Archbishop of Sydney</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_wealth">Personal wealth</span></h3>
<p>In 2005, the combined net worth of Malcolm and Lucy Turnbull was estimated at <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>133&#160;million,<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> making him Australia's richest parliamentarian<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> until the election of billionaire <a href="/wiki/Clive_Palmer" title="Clive Palmer">Clive Palmer</a> in the 2013 election.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Turnbull made the <i><a href="/wiki/BRW_Rich_200" class="mw-redirect" title="BRW Rich 200">BRW Rich 200</a></i> list for the second year running in 2010, and although he slipped from 182 to 197, his estimated net worth increased to <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>186&#160;million, and he continued to be the only sitting politician to make the list.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull was not listed in the 2014 list of the <i>BRW Rich 200</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> As of 2015, his estimated net worth is in excess of <span style="white-space: nowrap">A$</span>200&#160;million.<sup id="cite_ref-Stensholt_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stensholt-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span></h2>
<p>On 1 January 2001, Turnbull received the <a href="/wiki/Centenary_Medal" title="Centenary Medal">Centenary Medal</a> for services to the corporate sector.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/2021_Australia_Day_Honours" title="2021 Australia Day Honours">2021 Australia Day Honours</a>, he was awarded <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Companion of the Order of Australia</a> for "eminent service to the people and Parliament of Australia, particularly as Prime Minister, through significant contributions to national security, free trade, the environment and clean energy, innovation, economic reform and marriage equality, and to business and philanthropy".<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Published_works">Published works</span></h2>
<p>Turnbull has written several books:
